Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that for a long time now I’ve just had this kind of heavy, low feeling sitting on me most days. I still do what I’m supposed to do — I get through my routes, I show up, I handle my work, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts — but it feels like I’m just kind of moving through everything on autopilot.

But inside it’s like… I don’t know, like I’m not really present in my own life. I’m there, but I’m not really there. Just going through the motions, day after day, with this low feeling hanging over me. That’s what finally made me think maybe I should talk to somebody.

Yeah… I think that’s a fair way to put it, mostly. I don’t know if I’d use the word “emptiness” every single time, but there’s definitely this sadness, or heaviness, that’s just kind of there most days.

Like I said, I still do my routes, I still show up where I’m supposed to, and I still go to my volunteer shifts. I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.

Even when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. So yeah… most days, that heaviness doesn’t really go away.

Yeah… I’d say it’s there most days, for a good part of the day. I don’t know if I’m aware of it every single minute, because when I’m driving or dealing with customers or just trying to get through a route, I can sort of focus on what’s in front of me. It’s that same heavy, low feeling I was talking about — like I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, I’m showing up, I’m getting through work, I still make it to volunteering, but I’m not really feeling like I’m fully in my own life. More like I’m just moving from one thing to the next because that’s what I do.

Some days it’s stronger than others. Some days it’s more like a dull weight, and other days it feels harder to ignore. But yeah, overall, it’s pretty much there nearly every day in some form.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… about three years now, I’d say. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.

At first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.

So yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.

I still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.

But if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.

So… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.

I still do what I’m supposed to do — I get through my routes, I show up, I handle my work, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts — but it feels like I’m just kind of moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I stopped functioning completely or anything. From the outside, I probably look like I’m doing alright, because I keep going and I don’t really make a big scene about it. But inside it’s like… I don’t know, like I’m not really present in my own life. I’m there, but I’m not really there. I wouldn’t say everything is meaningless. It’s just that with the way I’ve been feeling overall, sometimes it takes more effort to get into the right frame of mind, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a big change where things I used to like suddenly don’t matter to me at all. That doesn’t quite sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel a sadness or emptiness that doesn’t go away, even after doing things that used to feel meaningful or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a fair way to put it, mostly. I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.\n\nEven when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.

But it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.

It’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.

So yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.

I still do what I’m supposed to do — I get through my routes, I show up, I handle my work, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts — but it feels like I’m just kind of moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I stopped functioning completely or anything. From the outside, I probably look like I’m doing alright, because I keep going and I don’t really make a big scene about it. But inside it’s like… I don’t know, like I’m not really present in my own life. I’m there, but I’m not really there. Just going through the motions, day after day, with this low feeling hanging over me. That’s what finally made me think maybe I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that activities you once looked forward to now feel uninteresting or meaningless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that exactly. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve had some clear moment where I thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nLike, some things still feel okay once I’m there or once I get started. I wouldn’t say everything is meaningless. It’s just that with the way I’ve been feeling overall, sometimes it takes more effort to get into the right frame of mind, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a big change where things I used to like suddenly don’t matter to me at all. That doesn’t quite sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel a sadness or emptiness that doesn’t go away, even after doing things that used to feel meaningful or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a fair way to put it, mostly. I don’t know if I’d use the word “emptiness” every single time, but there’s definitely this sadness, or heaviness, that’s just kind of there most days.\n\nLike I said, I still do my routes, I still show up where I’m supposed to, and I still go to my volunteer shifts. I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.\n\nEven when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. So yeah… most days, that heaviness doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or sadness is present most of the day, nearly every day, even if it varies in intensity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s there most days, for a good part of the day. I don’t know if I’m aware of it every single minute, because when I’m driving or dealing with customers or just trying to get through a route, I can sort of focus on what’s in front of me. But even then, it feels like it’s in the background.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, low feeling I was talking about — like I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, I’m showing up, I’m getting through work, I still make it to volunteering, but I’m not really feeling like I’m fully in my own life. More like I’m just moving from one thing to the next because that’s what I do.\n\nSome days it’s stronger than others. Some days it’s more like a dull weight, and other days it feels harder to ignore. But yeah, overall, it’s pretty much there nearly every day in some form.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this low mood or heaviness that you’ve been describing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years now, I’d say. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.\n\nSo yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or feel interested in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you feel good or connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.\n\nI still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.

Most mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.

There are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.

Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel a lack of energy or fatigue most days, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.\n\nMost mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.\n\nThere are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not worth much, even about things that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Low_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I keep comparing myself to the kind of man my dad was, and even though nobody’s telling me I’m failing, I hear it in my own head that I should be further along and handling things better than this.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that one hits a little closer, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.

I catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”

Nobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.

And yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.

I still do what I’m supposed to do — I get through my routes, I show up, I handle my work, and I still make it to my volunteer shifts — but it feels like I’m just kind of moving through everything on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I stopped functioning completely or anything. From the outside, I probably look like I’m doing alright, because I keep going and I don’t really make a big scene about it. But inside it’s like… I don’t know, like I’m not really present in my own life. I’m there, but I’m not really there. Just going through the motions, day after day, with this low feeling hanging over me. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve had some clear moment where I thought, “I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nLike, some things still feel okay once I’m there or once I get started. I wouldn’t say everything is meaningless. It’s just that with the way I’ve been feeling overall, sometimes it takes more effort to get into the right frame of mind, if that makes sense. That doesn’t quite sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel a sadness or emptiness that doesn’t go away, even after doing things that used to feel meaningful or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a fair way to put it, mostly. I don’t know if I’d use the word “emptiness” every single time, but there’s definitely this sadness, or heaviness, that’s just kind of there most days.\n\nLike I said, I still do my routes, I still show up where I’m supposed to, and I still go to my volunteer shifts. I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.\n\nEven when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. So yeah… most days, that heaviness doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or sadness is present most of the day, nearly every day, even if it varies in intensity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s there most days, for a good part of the day. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.\n\nSo yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or feel interested in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you feel good or connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.\n\nI still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.

I mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.

But I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. It’s hard to separate that from “not enjoying things,” if that makes sense.

So no, I wouldn’t say I can’t feel pleasure from anything. I still can. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.

That doesn’t quite sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days you feel a sadness or emptiness that doesn’t go away, even after doing things that used to feel meaningful or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a fair way to put it, mostly. I don’t know if I’d use the word “emptiness” every single time, but there’s definitely this sadness, or heaviness, that’s just kind of there most days.\n\nLike I said, I still do my routes, I still show up where I’m supposed to, and I still go to my volunteer shifts. I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.\n\nEven when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. So yeah… most days, that heaviness doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or sadness is present most of the day, nearly every day, even if it varies in intensity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s there most days, for a good part of the day. I don’t know if I’m aware of it every single minute, because when I’m driving or dealing with customers or just trying to get through a route, I can sort of focus on what’s in front of me. But even then, it feels like it’s in the background.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, low feeling I was talking about — like I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, I’m showing up, I’m getting through work, I still make it to volunteering, but I’m not really feeling like I’m fully in my own life. More like I’m just moving from one thing to the next because that’s what I do.\n\nSome days it’s stronger than others. Some days it’s more like a dull weight, and other days it feels harder to ignore. But yeah, overall, it’s pretty much there nearly every day in some form.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this low mood or heaviness that you’ve been describing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years now, I’d say. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.\n\nSo yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or feel interested in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you feel good or connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.\n\nI still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel a lack of energy or fatigue most days, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.\n\nMost mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.\n\nThere are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not worth much, even about things that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one hits a little closer, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.\n\nI catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”\n\nNobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.\n\nAnd yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things you used to enjoy — even small things — that you don’t feel any pleasure from anymore, like you can’t really feel joy in anything at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.\n\nI mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.\n\nBut I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. It’s hard to separate that from “not enjoying things,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I can’t feel pleasure from anything. I still can. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, maybe? But I don’t know that I’ve really measured it against how it used to be.

Like, if I sit down and have a decent dinner, or watch something I like, I can still enjoy it in the moment. It’s not fake, exactly. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This does nothing for me.” But if you’re asking whether it feels as strong as it did years ago… I guess I’d have to think about what “strong” even means.

When I was younger, maybe things felt more exciting because life was different. You had more to look forward to, or things felt newer. Now a lot of things are just routine. So I don’t know if the pleasure is less because something’s wrong with me, or because I’m 50 and life is kind of quieter and more repetitive.

I wouldn’t say it’s noticeably gone down in a way I’m clearly aware of. Not like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more that I don’t pay that much attention to it. I still get some enjoyment from things. Maybe it’s not fireworks, but I’m not sure it ever was supposed to be.

More like I’m just moving from one thing to the next because that’s what I do.\n\nSome days it’s stronger than others. Some days it’s more like a dull weight, and other days it feels harder to ignore. But yeah, overall, it’s pretty much there nearly every day in some form.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this low mood or heaviness that you’ve been describing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years now, I’d say. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.\n\nSo yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or feel interested in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you feel good or connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.\n\nI still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel a lack of energy or fatigue most days, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.\n\nMost mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.\n\nThere are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not worth much, even about things that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one hits a little closer, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.\n\nI catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”\n\nNobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.\n\nAnd yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things you used to enjoy — even small things — that you don’t feel any pleasure from anymore, like you can’t really feel joy in anything at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.\n\nI mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.\n\nBut I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. It’s hard to separate that from “not enjoying things,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I can’t feel pleasure from anything. I still can. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the pleasure you feel from the things you still enjoy is noticeably less than it used to be, even if it’s not completely gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, maybe? But I don’t know that I’ve really measured it against how it used to be.\n\nLike, if I sit down and have a decent dinner, or watch something I like, I can still enjoy it in the moment. It’s not fake, exactly. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This does nothing for me.” But if you’re asking whether it feels as strong as it did years ago… I guess I’d have to think about what “strong” even means.\n\nWhen I was younger, maybe things felt more exciting because life was different. You had more to look forward to, or things felt newer. Now a lot of things are just routine. So I don’t know if the pleasure is less because something’s wrong with me, or because I’m 50 and life is kind of quieter and more repetitive.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s noticeably gone down in a way I’m clearly aware of. Not like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more that I don’t pay that much attention to it. I still get some enjoyment from things. Maybe it’s not fireworks, but I’m not sure it ever was supposed to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.\n\nI catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”\n\nNobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.\n\nAnd yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things you used to enjoy — even small things — that you don’t feel any pleasure from anymore, like you can’t really feel joy in anything at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.\n\nI mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.\n\nBut I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the pleasure you feel from the things you still enjoy is noticeably less than it used to be, even if it’s not completely gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, maybe? But I don’t know that I’ve really measured it against how it used to be.\n\nLike, if I sit down and have a decent dinner, or watch something I like, I can still enjoy it in the moment. It’s not fake, exactly. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This does nothing for me.” But if you’re asking whether it feels as strong as it did years ago… I guess I’d have to think about what “strong” even means.\n\nWhen I was younger, maybe things felt more exciting because life was different. You had more to look forward to, or things felt newer. Now a lot of things are just routine. So I don’t know if the pleasure is less because something’s wrong with me, or because I’m 50 and life is kind of quieter and more repetitive.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s noticeably gone down in a way I’m clearly aware of. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d say “most things,” no. Or at least… I’m not sure. That sounds bigger than what I notice in myself.

I mean, my life isn’t exactly packed with hobbies and excitement, so sometimes it’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether I just don’t have a whole lot going on outside the usual routine. Work, errands, eating dinner, maybe watching something, trying to unwind. That kind of thing. So if you asked me to list all these things I used to be passionate about and now don’t care about, I don’t know that I could do that.

There are probably things I don’t go out of my way for as much anymore, but I also don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest, or because I’m tired, or because it feels like too much effort to plan anything after a long day. And I can still get interested in things here and there. If something catches my attention, I can follow it. I’m not totally shut off from everything.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in most things. Maybe my interests are kind of narrower or quieter than they used to be, but “most things” feels like too strong a way to put it.

I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel a lack of energy or fatigue most days, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.\n\nMost mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.\n\nThere are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not worth much, even about things that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one hits a little closer, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.\n\nI catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”\n\nNobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.\n\nAnd yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things you used to enjoy — even small things — that you don’t feel any pleasure from anymore, like you can’t really feel joy in anything at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.\n\nI mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.\n\nBut I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. It’s hard to separate that from “not enjoying things,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I can’t feel pleasure from anything. I still can. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the pleasure you feel from the things you still enjoy is noticeably less than it used to be, even if it’s not completely gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, maybe? But I don’t know that I’ve really measured it against how it used to be.\n\nLike, if I sit down and have a decent dinner, or watch something I like, I can still enjoy it in the moment. It’s not fake, exactly. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This does nothing for me.” But if you’re asking whether it feels as strong as it did years ago… I guess I’d have to think about what “strong” even means.\n\nWhen I was younger, maybe things felt more exciting because life was different. You had more to look forward to, or things felt newer. Now a lot of things are just routine. So I don’t know if the pleasure is less because something’s wrong with me, or because I’m 50 and life is kind of quieter and more repetitive.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s noticeably gone down in a way I’m clearly aware of. Not like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more that I don’t pay that much attention to it. I still get some enjoyment from things. Maybe it’s not fireworks, but I’m not sure it ever was supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most things, even if you still get some enjoyment out of a few of them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most things,” no. Or at least… I’m not sure. That sounds bigger than what I notice in myself.\n\nI mean, my life isn’t exactly packed with hobbies and excitement, so sometimes it’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether I just don’t have a whole lot going on outside the usual routine. Work, errands, eating dinner, maybe watching something, trying to unwind. That kind of thing. So if you asked me to list all these things I used to be passionate about and now don’t care about, I don’t know that I could do that.\n\nThere are probably things I don’t go out of my way for as much anymore, but I also don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest, or because I’m tired, or because it feels like too much effort to plan anything after a long day. And I can still get interested in things here and there. If something catches my attention, I can follow it. I’m not totally shut off from everything.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in most things. Maybe my interests are kind of narrower or quieter than they used to be, but “most things” feels like too strong a way to put it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Almost all activities? No, I don’t think I’d go that far. That feels like a pretty strong way to say it.

I still do care about some things, and I still get pulled into things now and then. It’s not like I’m just going through every activity with no interest at all. I mean, there are plenty of days where I’m tired or I don’t feel like making much effort, and maybe I end up doing the same easy things because they don’t take a lot out of me. But that’s different, to me, from saying I’ve lost interest in almost everything.

I guess there are things I used to do more often that I don’t really keep up with now, but I don’t always know if that’s because I stopped caring or because life just kind of narrowed down over time. You work, you come home, you handle what needs handling, and then there’s only so much energy left to go looking for activities. So maybe from the outside it might look like I don’t do much, but inside it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in almost all of it.

So… no, I wouldn’t say that. Maybe my interests are less active than they used to be, or I’m less motivated to go out of my way, but I still have interest in some things. “Almost all activities” sounds too much like everything has gone blank, and that’s not really how it feels.

I can get through all of it. Sometimes I even get distracted enough in the middle of doing something that I’m not thinking about it every second. But underneath it, there’s this low feeling sitting there, like it doesn’t really lift all the way.\n\nEven when I do things that should feel meaningful, I don’t come away feeling like, “Okay, I’m back to myself now.” It’s more like I did the thing, I got through it, maybe it was fine, but then I’m still me afterwards, still feeling like I’m just moving through the day instead of really being in it. So yeah… most days, that heaviness doesn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or sadness is present most of the day, nearly every day, even if it varies in intensity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s there most days, for a good part of the day. I don’t know if I’m aware of it every single minute, because when I’m driving or dealing with customers or just trying to get through a route, I can sort of focus on what’s in front of me. But even then, it feels like it’s in the background.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, low feeling I was talking about — like I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, I’m showing up, I’m getting through work, I still make it to volunteering, but I’m not really feeling like I’m fully in my own life. More like I’m just moving from one thing to the next because that’s what I do.\n\nSome days it’s stronger than others. But yeah, overall, it’s pretty much there nearly every day in some form.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this low mood or heaviness that you’ve been describing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years now, I’d say. It started after my father died. I mean, I don’t know if there was one exact day where it flipped on, but looking back, that’s when things changed.\n\nAt first I think I told myself it was just grief, you know, and maybe it was. I figured time would kind of straighten things out, or I’d get used to it and feel more like myself again. But the heaviness just sort of stayed around. It’s not like every day has been exactly the same for three years, but that low feeling has been there in some form since then.\n\nSo yeah… about three years, since my dad passed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer take pleasure or feel interested in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you feel good or connected?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I wouldn’t say everything feels good the way it maybe used to, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest either.\n\nI still do things. I still go through my routines, and if there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually show up. Sometimes while I’m doing it, it’s fine enough, you know? I can talk to people, I can get through it, I can even have moments where I’m distracted or it feels okay for a little while.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I get that same feeling from things — like feeling really lifted up by them, or connected in the way I used to — I guess I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate from just being tired of feeling the way I’ve been feeling. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Do I enjoy this less than before?” It’s more like I do the thing, and then afterwards I’m still kind of back where I was.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure I’d call it no pleasure or no interest. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it’s harder to focus, think clearly, or make decisions lately — like your mind feels foggy or you’re struggling to stay on top of things mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that some. Not like I can’t function, because I can still do my job and I’m not, you know, getting completely lost or anything like that. I know my routes, I know what needs to get done, and most days I get through it.\n\nBut it does feel like my mind doesn’t grab onto things as quickly as it used to. Like I’ll be looking at the delivery times and I’ll catch myself rereading the same line a couple times, just to make sure I actually took it in. Or I’ll stand there looking at the schedule board longer than I probably should, not because it’s confusing exactly, but because it’s like my brain is slow to click into it.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because the stuff isn’t new to me. I’ve done this kind of work a long time, so it’s not like I don’t know how to read the schedule or plan out what I’m doing. It’s more that I have to push myself to focus on it. Sometimes I’ll look at something and realize my eyes were on it but my mind wasn’t really there yet.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say there’s some fogginess or trouble locking in mentally, especially with the little details. I’m still managing, but it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel a lack of energy or fatigue most days, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. I mean, I do get tired, but I also work and I’m not 25 anymore, so sometimes it’s hard for me to tell what’s just normal tired versus something else.\n\nMost mornings I can get up and do what I need to do. I’m not bouncing out of bed or anything, but I wouldn’t say I’m lying there unable to move because I have no energy. And if I’ve slept okay, sometimes I feel… okay enough, I guess. Not great, but not totally wiped out either.\n\nThere are days where I feel kind of worn down, but I don’t know that I’ve really tracked whether that’s “most days” or whether it’s even after enough rest. Sometimes rest helps, sometimes I still feel kind of heavy, but I’m not sure I’d call it fatigue in the way you’re asking. I guess I haven’t noticed it as a main thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not worth much, even about things that wouldn’t have bothered you before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one hits a little closer, honestly. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly, like I did some one specific terrible thing and I’m beating myself up over it. It’s more like this steady voice in my head that says I ought to be doing better than I am.\n\nI catch myself comparing myself to my dad a lot. He was the kind of man who just… handled things. At least that’s how I remember him. Worked hard, didn’t complain much, seemed like he knew what he was supposed to do and just did it. And then I look at myself and think, “You’re 50 years old, you should be further along than this. You should have more figured out. You should be stronger about things.”\n\nNobody’s really saying that to me, which almost makes it harder to explain. It’s not like someone at work or in my family is standing there telling me I’m failing. It’s more me saying it to myself. Like even if I get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, there’s still this feeling that I’m not measuring up somehow.\n\nAnd yeah, maybe things bother me more than they used to because I turn them into some bigger judgment about myself. Like instead of just thinking, “That was a rough day,” my mind goes to, “Why can’t you handle this better?” or “Your dad would’ve just pushed through this.” I know that’s probably not fair, but it’s where my head goes. So… yeah, I’d say I do feel kind of low about myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are things you used to enjoy — even small things — that you don’t feel any pleasure from anymore, like you can’t really feel joy in anything at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly, like I can’t feel joy in anything at all. That sounds a little more extreme than what I’d say.\n\nI mean… I still have things I like. Little things, mostly. A decent meal, a quiet evening, maybe watching something familiar on TV, or just having a day where nothing goes wrong. I can still appreciate that stuff. It’s not like everything is completely flat or dead to me.\n\nBut I guess I’d have to think about it, because I don’t always get excited about things the way maybe I used to. Sometimes I wonder if that’s just getting older, or being tired from the routine, or just life being kind of… same thing over and over. It’s hard to separate that from “not enjoying things,” if that makes sense.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I can’t feel pleasure from anything. I still can. Maybe it’s quieter than it used to be, or I don’t seek things out as much, but I wouldn’t describe it as no joy at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the pleasure you feel from the things you still enjoy is noticeably less than it used to be, even if it’s not completely gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, maybe? But I don’t know that I’ve really measured it against how it used to be.\n\nLike, if I sit down and have a decent dinner, or watch something I like, I can still enjoy it in the moment. It’s not fake, exactly. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This does nothing for me.” But if you’re asking whether it feels as strong as it did years ago… I guess I’d have to think about what “strong” even means.\n\nWhen I was younger, maybe things felt more exciting because life was different. You had more to look forward to, or things felt newer. Now a lot of things are just routine. So I don’t know if the pleasure is less because something’s wrong with me, or because I’m 50 and life is kind of quieter and more repetitive.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s noticeably gone down in a way I’m clearly aware of. Not like, “Oh, I used to love this and now I feel nothing.” It’s more that I don’t pay that much attention to it. I still get some enjoyment from things. Maybe it’s not fireworks, but I’m not sure it ever was supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most things, even if you still get some enjoyment out of a few of them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “most things,” no. Or at least… I’m not sure. That sounds bigger than what I notice in myself.\n\nI mean, my life isn’t exactly packed with hobbies and excitement, so sometimes it’s hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest or whether I just don’t have a whole lot going on outside the usual routine. Work, errands, eating dinner, maybe watching something, trying to unwind. That kind of thing. So if you asked me to list all these things I used to be passionate about and now don’t care about, I don’t know that I could do that.\n\nThere are probably things I don’t go out of my way for as much anymore, but I also don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest, or because I’m tired, or because it feels like too much effort to plan anything after a long day. And I can still get interested in things here and there. If something catches my attention, I can follow it. I’m not totally shut off from everything.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in most things. Maybe my interests are kind of narrower or quieter than they used to be, but “most things” feels like too strong a way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to care about, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Almost all activities? No, I don’t think I’d go that far. That feels like a pretty strong way to say it.\n\nI still do care about some things, and I still get pulled into things now and then. It’s not like I’m just going through every activity with no interest at all. I mean, there are plenty of days where I’m tired or I don’t feel like making much effort, and maybe I end up doing the same easy things because they don’t take a lot out of me. But that’s different, to me, from saying I’ve lost interest in almost everything.\n\nI guess there are things I used to do more often that I don’t really keep up with now, but I don’t always know if that’s because I stopped caring or because life just kind of narrowed down over time. You work, you come home, you handle what needs handling, and then there’s only so much energy left to go looking for activities. So maybe from the outside it might look like I don’t do much, but inside it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in almost all of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that. Maybe my interests are less active than they used to be, or I’m less motivated to go out of my way, but I still have interest in some things. “Almost all activities” sounds too much like everything has gone blank, and that’s not really how it feels.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F33.1", "F33.2", "F33.8", "F33.9"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking it a little differently, but I think my answer is still… not really, at least not in the sense of “almost everything.”

I do have times where I don’t feel like doing much, and I probably let things slide more than I used to. But if you’re asking whether I’ve basically lost interest across the board, even in things that mattered to me before, I don’t think I can honestly say that. It’s not like I look at everything and feel nothing, or I’m just forcing myself through activities I don’t care about anymore.

It’s more like my life has gotten narrower, maybe. I stick to the same routines. I don’t always seek things out. Sometimes it feels easier to just do what’s familiar than to make the effort to do something different. But that feels different from losing interest in almost everything. I can still care about things, I can still get into something if it catches me, and I still have some enjoyment there.

So… no, I wouldn’t say “almost everything.” That sounds more severe than what I experience.

But that’s different, to me, from saying I’ve lost interest in almost everything.\n\nI guess there are things I used to do more often that I don’t really keep up with now, but I don’t always know if that’s because I stopped caring or because life just kind of narrowed down over time. You work, you come home, you handle what needs handling, and then there’s only so much energy left to go looking for activities. So maybe from the outside it might look like I don’t do much, but inside it doesn’t feel like I’ve lost interest in almost all of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say that. Maybe my interests are less active than they used to be, or I’m less motivated to go out of my way, but I still have interest in some things. “Almost all activities” sounds too much like everything has gone blank, and that’s not really how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest in almost everything, including things you used to care about, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it a little differently, but I think my answer is still… not really, at least not in the sense of “almost everything.”\n\nI do have times where I don’t feel like doing much, and I probably let things slide more than I used to. But if you’re asking whether I’ve basically lost interest across the board, even in things that mattered to me before, I don’t think I can honestly say that. It’s not like I look at everything and feel nothing, or I’m just forcing myself through activities I don’t care about anymore.\n\nIt’s more like my life has gotten narrower, maybe. I stick to the same routines. I don’t always seek things out. Sometimes it feels easier to just do what’s familiar than to make the effort to do something different. But that feels different from losing interest in almost everything. I can still care about things, I can still get into something if it catches me, and I still have some enjoyment there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say “almost everything.” That sounds more severe than what I experience.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
